where k is the soliton wave number. In the continuum limit this reduces to the KP dispersion ω = k³. The discrete KP hierarchy also admits rational solutions (lump solutions) and periodic solutions expressed in terms of Riemann theta functions on a discrete spectral curve.

Integrable Boundary Conditions for the Vector Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation

Integrable boundary conditions for the vector NLS on the half‑line x ≥ 0 are characterised by a reflection matrix K(k) that satisfies the boundary Yang–Baxter equation

R₁₂(k₁,k₂) K₁(k₁) R₂₁(k₂,-k₁) K₂(k₂) = K₂(k₂) R₁₂(k₂,-k₁) K₁(k₁) R₂₁(-k₁,-k₂),

where R is the bulk R‑matrix of the AKNS system. For the Manakov model the most general diagonal K‑matrix depends on two boundary parameters and reads

K(k) = diag( (k - i α)/(k + i α), (k - i β)/(k + i β), (k - i β)/(k + i β) ).

The direct scattering problem on the half‑line uses the Jost solution that satisfies the boundary condition Ψₓ(0,t) = K Ψ(0,t). The scattering data consist of a reflection matrix R(k) and a set of bound states with norming vectors. The time evolution of the scattering data is identical to the whole‑line case. The inverse problem is formulated as a Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real k‑axis with a jump matrix that incorporates the reflection matrix.

Soliton reflection at the boundary is described by the boundary scattering matrix. For a one‑soliton incident on the boundary, the reflected soliton acquires a phase shift and its polarisation vector is transformed by a unitary matrix that depends on the boundary parameters α, β. This polarisation rotation is a distinctive feature of the vector model and has no scalar analogue. The boundary conditions preserving integrability for the higher‑order vector NLS (Hirota, Sasa–Satsuma) are obtained by requiring the same K‑matrix to satisfy the boundary Yang–Baxter equation with the higher‑order R‑matrix.

  • Confusing the polarisation vector of a Manakov soliton with a scalar amplitude leads to missing the unitary rotation during collisions.
  • Assuming the Hirota equation is integrable for arbitrary coefficients; the integrability condition β = 2α must hold.
  • Overlooking the quartet symmetry of eigenvalues in reverse‑space‑time nonlocal equations and using only pairs as in the local case.
  • Treating the discrete KP equation as a mere discretisation of KP without recognising its role as the master equation for the whole hierarchy.
  • Neglecting the boundary Yang–Baxter equation when constructing integrable boundary conditions for vector models, resulting in non‑integrable boundary terms.
  • Misidentifying the Pfaffian tau function of the discrete BKP hierarchy with the determinant tau function of the discrete CKP hierarchy.
  • Forgetting that the Sasa–Satsuma equation includes a self‑steepening term that modifies the soliton phase by a tanh‑dependent contribution.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Korteweg–de Vries Equation

The nonlocal Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation appears in two principal forms: the reverse‑space‑time nonlocal KdV, ut(x,t) + uxxx(x,t) + 6 u(x,t) ux(-x,-t) = 0, and the reverse‑space nonlocal KdV, ut(x,t) + uxxx(x,t) + 6 u(x,t) ux(-x,t) = 0. Both were introduced by Ablowitz and Musslimani as integrable nonlocal reductions of the standard KdV equation. The Lax pair for the reverse‑space‑time case uses the same spatial operator L = -∂x2 + u(x,t) as the local KdV, but the time evolution operator is modified to B = -4∂x3 + 6 u(x,t) ∂x + 3 ux(x,t) + 3 u(-x,-t) ∂x + 3 ux(-x,-t). The compatibility condition Lt = [B, L] yields the nonlocal equation. The direct scattering problem is formulated for the Schrödinger operator L on the whole line with potential u(x,0). Because of the nonlocal reduction, the potential satisfies u(x,0) = u(-x,0) for the reverse‑space case, or u(x,0) = u(-x,0) with an additional time reversal for the reverse‑space‑time case. This symmetry forces the scattering data to obey specific constraints: the reflection coefficient R(k) satisfies R(-k) = R(k) for the reverse‑space reduction, and R(-k) = R*(k) for the reverse‑space‑time reduction. Bound states occur in symmetric pairs (k, -k) with norming constants related by the same symmetries. The inverse problem is solved via a Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real k‑axis with a jump matrix that incorporates these symmetries. One‑soliton solutions are obtained by solving the Riemann–Hilbert problem with a single pair of poles. For the reverse‑space‑time nonlocal KdV, the one‑soliton solution takes the form u(x,t) = 2 η2 sech[2 η (x - 4 ξ t) + δ] where the phase shift δ depends on the norming constant and the nonlocal symmetry forces the soliton to be centered at x = 0 when t = 0. Multi‑soliton solutions are constructed by the dressing method or by solving the associated algebraic system. An important feature is that the nonlocal equation admits solutions that blow up in finite time for certain choices of norming constants, a phenomenon absent in the local KdV. The long‑time asymptotics of the nonlocal KdV can be analysed by the Deift–Zhou steepest descent method adapted to the symmetric jump contour, leading to a soliton resolution conjecture similar to the local case but with modified phase shifts due to the nonlocal interaction.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Modified Korteweg–de Vries Equation

The nonlocal modified KdV (mKdV) equation, ut(x,t) + uxxx(x,t) - 6 u(x,t)2 ux(-x,-t) = 0, is the reverse‑space‑time nonlocal reduction of the focusing mKdV equation. Its Lax pair is based on the AKNS system with a 2×2 matrix spectral problem: Ψx = U Ψ, U = -i k σ3 + Q, where Q = [[0, u(x,t)], [u(-x,-t), 0]] and σ3 = diag(1, -1). The time part is Ψt = V Ψ with V a polynomial in k of degree three whose coefficients involve u and its derivatives. The compatibility condition yields the nonlocal mKdV equation. The direct scattering problem is defined for the AKNS system on the real line. The nonlocal reduction imposes the symmetry Q(x) = σ2 Q†(-x) σ2 (for the reverse‑space‑time case) which translates into symmetries of the Jost solutions and the scattering matrix S(k). Specifically, the scattering matrix satisfies S(-k) = σ2 S†(k) σ2. This forces the reflection coefficients to obey r1(-k) = -r2*(k) and the transmission coefficient to satisfy a(-k) = a*(k). Discrete eigenvalues appear in quartets: if k is an eigenvalue, then -k, k*, -k* are also eigenvalues. The inverse problem is formulated as a Riemann–Hilbert problem with a jump matrix on the real axis that respects these symmetries. One‑soliton solutions correspond to a single quartet of simple poles. The solution can be written in terms of hyperbolic functions: u(x,t) = 2 η sech[2 η (x - 4 (ξ2 - η2) t) + δ] exp[i (2 ξ x - 4 ξ (ξ2 - 3 η2) t + φ)], where the parameters satisfy the symmetry constraints. The nonlocal mKdV also admits breather solutions (bound states of two solitons) which are periodic in time and localized in space. The interaction of two solitons is elastic, but the phase shifts differ from the local mKdV due to the nonlocal coupling. The Cauchy problem for decaying initial data can be solved globally in time for generic data, but finite‑time blow‑up can occur for special symmetric initial conditions. The long‑time asymptotics are again accessible via the Deift–Zhou method, with the symmetric contour leading to a modified g‑function.

Worked Example: Symmetry of Scattering Data for Nonlocal KdV
Consider the reverse‑space‑time nonlocal KdV equation ut + uxxx + 6 u(x,t) ux(-x,-t) = 0. The associated Lax pair uses the Schrödinger operator L = -∂x2 + u(x,t). Show that if u(x,0) satisfies u(x,0) = u(-x,0), then the reflection coefficient R(k) for the scattering problem L ψ = k2 ψ satisfies R(-k) = R*(k).
The scattering problem is -ψxx + u(x) ψ = k2 ψ. Let ψ±(x,k) be the Jost solutions with asymptotics ψ± ∼ e±i k x as x → ±∞. The scattering matrix relates them: ψ-(x,k) = a(k) ψ+(x,-k) + b(k) ψ+(x,k). The reflection coefficient is R(k) = b(k)/a(k). The potential symmetry u(x) = u(-x) implies that if ψ(x,k) is a solution, then ψ(-x,k) is also a solution. Applying this to the Jost solutions gives ψ±(-x,k) = ψ∓(x,k). Using the definition of the scattering matrix, we find a(-k) = a(k) and b(-k) = b*(k). Hence R(-k) = b(-k)/a(-k) = b*(k)/a(k) = R*(k). This symmetry is crucial for the formulation of the inverse problem as a Riemann–Hilbert problem with a symmetric jump contour.
Exam Strategy: Nonlocal Integrable Systems
When a question asks for the inverse scattering transform of a nonlocal equation, first identify the type of nonlocality (reverse‑space, reverse‑time, or reverse‑space‑time). Write down the Lax pair and the symmetry condition on the potential. Derive the induced symmetries on the scattering matrix (reflection/transmission coefficients, discrete eigenvalues, norming constants). The inverse problem is always a Riemann–Hilbert problem with a jump matrix that respects these symmetries. For soliton solutions, remember that discrete eigenvalues appear in symmetric sets (pairs for reverse‑space, quartets for reverse‑space‑time). The one‑soliton formula is obtained by solving the residue conditions at these poles. Examiners often test the symmetry of the scattering data and the structure of the Riemann–Hilbert problem rather than the full asymptotic analysis.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Sine‑Gordon Equation

The nonlocal sine‑Gordon equation, uxt(x,t) = sin u(-x,-t), is a reverse‑space‑time nonlocal reduction of the classical sine‑Gordon equation. Its Lax pair is a 2×2 AKNS‑type system with spectral parameter λ: Ψx = U Ψ, U = (i/2) (λ - λ-1) σ3 + (i/4) (ux σ1 + ut σ2), and Ψt = V Ψ with V having a similar structure. The nonlocal reduction imposes u(x,t) = u(-x,-t), which translates into the symmetry U(x,t,λ) = -σ2 U†(-x,-t,λ*) σ2. The direct scattering problem is formulated on the whole line for the x‑part at t=0. The Jost solutions satisfy symmetry relations that force the scattering matrix S(λ) to obey S(λ) = σ2 S†(λ*) σ2. Consequently, the reflection coefficients satisfy r1(λ) = -r2*(λ*) and the transmission coefficient a(λ) satisfies a(λ) = a*(λ*). Discrete eigenvalues (bound states) appear in quartets: λ, -λ, λ*, -λ*. The inverse problem is a Riemann–Hilbert problem on the unit circle (or the real line after a Möbius transformation) with a jump matrix incorporating these symmetries. One‑soliton solutions correspond to a single quartet of simple poles. The solution can be expressed in terms of the Bäcklund transformation: u(x,t) = 4 arctan[exp(η (x - v t) + δ)] where the parameters are constrained by the nonlocal symmetry. The nonlocal sine‑Gordon equation also admits breather solutions (bound states of a soliton and an antisoliton) which are periodic in time. The interaction of two solitons is elastic, but the phase shifts are modified by the nonlocal coupling. The Cauchy problem for initial data decaying to zero at infinity can be solved globally, and the long‑time asymptotics show soliton resolution with a finite number of solitons and a decaying dispersive tail.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Boussinesq Equation

The nonlocal Boussinesq equation, utt - uxx - 3 (u2)xx - uxxxx = 0 with the nonlocal reduction u(x,t) = u(-x,-t), is an integrable nonlocal generalization of the classical Boussinesq equation. Its Lax pair is a 3×3 matrix system (or a 2×2 system after a reduction) with a spectral parameter k. The spatial part is Ψx = U Ψ, where U is a polynomial in k of degree one, and the time part is Ψt = V Ψ with V a polynomial of degree two. The nonlocal reduction imposes a symmetry on U and V that leads to constraints on the scattering data. The direct scattering problem is defined for the spatial operator on the real line. The Jost solutions are 3‑component vectors, and the scattering matrix S(k) is 3×3. The symmetry u(x) = u(-x) forces S(k) to satisfy S(-k) = P ST(k) P-1 for a constant permutation matrix P. This implies that the reflection coefficients (which are now matrices) obey specific transpose relations, and the discrete eigenvalues appear in pairs (k, -k). The inverse problem is formulated as a matrix Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real axis. One‑soliton solutions are obtained by solving the residue conditions for a single pair of poles. The solution can be written in terms of exponential functions and exhibits a peakon‑like shape for certain parameter choices. Multi‑soliton solutions are constructed by the dressing method. The nonlocal Boussinesq equation also admits rational solutions (lumps) and periodic solutions expressed via Riemann theta functions. The Cauchy problem for decaying initial data is well‑posed, and the long‑time asymptotics can be analysed by the Deift–Zhou method adapted to the matrix Riemann–Hilbert problem with symmetric jump contour.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Kadomtsev–Petviashvili Equation

The nonlocal Kadomtsev–Petviashvili (KP) equation, (ut + 6 u ux + uxxx)x + 3 uyy = 0 with the nonlocal reduction u(x,y,t) = u(-x,-y,-t), is a (2+1)‑dimensional integrable nonlocal system. Its Lax pair consists of two linear equations: (∂y - ∂x2 - u) ψ = 0 and (∂t - 4 ∂x3 - 6 u ∂x - 3 ux - 3 ∂x-1 uy) ψ = 0. The nonlocal reduction imposes a symmetry on the potential u that translates into symmetries of the scattering data for the associated non‑stationary Schrödinger operator. The direct scattering problem is formulated for the operator L = ∂y - ∂x2 - u(x,y) at t=0. The Jost solutions are defined for fixed y as functions of x and a spectral parameter k. The scattering data consist of a reflection coefficient R(k,y) and a set of discrete eigenvalues with norming functions. The nonlocal symmetry forces R(-k,-y) = R*(k,y) and the discrete eigenvalues to appear in quartets (k, -k, k*, -k*). The inverse problem is a Riemann–Hilbert problem in the complex k‑plane with a jump matrix that depends on y and respects these symmetries. One‑soliton solutions are line solitons localized in the direction transverse to propagation. The solution takes the form u(x,y,t) = 2 η2 sech2[η (x + p y - (4 η2 + p2) t) + δ] where p is the transverse wave number. The nonlocal reduction forces the soliton to be symmetric with respect to the origin at t=0. Multi‑soliton solutions describe the interaction of line solitons, which is elastic but with modified phase shifts. The KP equation also admits lump solutions (rationally localized in both x and y) which in the nonlocal case must satisfy the symmetry condition. The Cauchy problem for decaying initial data can be solved globally, and the long‑time asymptotics show soliton resolution with a finite number of line solitons and a decaying dispersive tail.

  • Confusing the symmetry of the scattering data for reverse‑space nonlocality (pairs) with that for reverse‑space‑time nonlocality (quartets) leads to an incorrect Riemann–Hilbert formulation.
  • Assuming the nonlocal KdV equation has the same one‑soliton formula as the local KdV without the phase shift imposed by the nonlocal symmetry.
  • Neglecting the quartet structure of discrete eigenvalues in the nonlocal mKdV and nonlocal sine‑Gordon equations, which is essential for constructing real‑valued soliton solutions.
  • Treating the nonlocal Boussinesq equation as a scalar Riemann–Hilbert problem instead of a matrix problem, missing the 3×3 structure of the scattering matrix.
  • Overlooking the fact that the nonlocal KP equation requires the symmetry u(x,y,t) = u(-x,-y,-t) for integrability; using only u(x,y,t) = u(-x,-y,t) breaks the Lax pair compatibility.
  • Forgetting that the nonlocal sine‑Gordon equation admits breather solutions only when the discrete eigenvalues form complex conjugate pairs within the quartet.
  • Misapplying the Deift–Zhou steepest descent method to nonlocal equations without modifying the g‑function to respect the symmetric jump contour.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with Nonzero Boundary Conditions

The nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation with reverse‑space reduction, i qt + qxx + 2 q² q(−x) = 0, was introduced by Ablowitz and Musslimani in 2013. When the field approaches a non‑zero constant background q(x) → q₀ eiθ as |x| → ∞, the direct scattering problem is formulated for the Zakharov–Shabat system with a non‑vanishing asymptotic potential. The Jost solutions are defined on a two‑sheeted Riemann surface of the spectral parameter k with branch points at ± q₀. The nonlocal symmetry q(x) = q(−x) forces the scattering matrix S(k) to satisfy S(−k) = P ST(k) P−1, where P is the permutation matrix exchanging the two components. Consequently the reflection coefficient r(k) obeys r(−k) = − r*(k) and discrete eigenvalues appear in symmetric pairs (k, −k) on the physical sheet. The inverse problem is a matrix Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real axis together with the branch cut connecting the two sheets. The jump matrix incorporates the modified reflection coefficient and the exponential factor e2i(kx+2k²t) multiplied by the background phase. One‑soliton solutions on a non‑zero background are dark‑bright solitons whose intensity dip is centred at the origin at t = 0, a direct consequence of the reverse‑space symmetry. Multi‑soliton solutions are generated by the dressing method using a rational dressing factor that respects the symmetry of the poles. Long‑time asymptotics for decaying perturbations of the background are obtained by the Deift–Zhou steepest‑descent method adapted to the symmetric contour; the g‑function is constructed to be odd, ensuring the jump matrix decays exponentially away from the stationary phase points. The nonlocal NLS with non‑zero boundary conditions also admits rogue‑wave solutions expressed through higher‑order poles in the Riemann–Hilbert formulation, and their dynamics preserve the nonlocal symmetry at all times.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Modified Korteweg–de Vries Equation

The nonlocal modified KdV (mKdV) equation, ut + uxxx + 6 u² ux = 0 with the reverse‑space reduction u(x) = u(−x), possesses a Lax pair built from the AKNS 2 × 2 system with a spectral parameter λ. The spatial operator is Ψx = U Ψ, U = −i λ σ₃ + Q, where Q = (0 u; u 0) and σ₃ is the Pauli matrix. The nonlocal reduction imposes Q(x) = σ₂ Qᵀ(−x) σ₂, which translates into the symmetry S(−λ) = σ₂ Sᵀ(λ) σ₂ for the scattering matrix. As a result the reflection coefficient satisfies r(−λ) = − r*(λ) and the discrete spectrum consists of quartets (λ, −λ, λ*, −λ*) when u is real‑valued. The inverse problem is a 2 × 2 matrix Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real axis with jump matrix built from r(λ) and the exponential e2i(λx+4λ³t). One‑soliton solutions are obtained by solving the residue conditions for a single quartet; the resulting profile is a sech‑shaped pulse centred at the origin at t = 0, with amplitude and velocity determined by the imaginary part of λ. Multi‑soliton solutions are constructed by successive dressing transformations that preserve the quartet structure. The nonlocal mKdV also admits breather solutions when the quartet contains a complex conjugate pair off the imaginary axis; these breathers are symmetric with respect to the spatial origin. The long‑time asymptotic analysis follows the Deift–Zhou method with a g‑function that is odd in λ, guaranteeing that the jump matrix respects the symmetric contour. An important subtlety is that the nonlocal reduction eliminates the possibility of pure imaginary discrete eigenvalues that would correspond to standard solitons in the local mKdV; only quartets are allowed, which modifies the soliton interaction phase shifts compared with the local case.

Worked Example: Symmetry of Scattering Data for Nonlocal NLS
Consider the nonlocal NLS equation i q_t + q_xx + 2 q2 q(-x) = 0 with vanishing boundary conditions. The associated Zakharov–Shabat system is Ψ_x = U Ψ, U = -i k σ₃ + Q, Q = (0 q; q(-x) 0). Show that the scattering matrix S(k) satisfies S(-k) = P S^T(k) P-1 with P = (0 1; 1 0).
The spatial Lax operator is L = ∂_x - U. Define the Jost solutions Ψ_±(x,k) by their asymptotics Ψ_± ~ e-i k x σ₃ as x → ±∞. Because Q(x) = σ₂ Q^T(-x) σ₂ with σ₂ = (0 -i; i 0), one verifies that if Ψ(x,k) solves Ψ_x = U Ψ, then σ₂ Ψ^T(-x,-k) σ₂ solves the same equation. Matching the asymptotics gives the relation between the scattering matrices: S(-k) = σ₂ S^T(k) σ₂. Since σ₂ = i P with P = (0 1; 1 0) and σ₂2 = -I, the relation simplifies to S(-k) = P S^T(k) P-1. This symmetry forces the reflection coefficient r(k) = b(k)/a(k) to satisfy r(-k) = -r^*(k) and the discrete eigenvalues to appear in pairs (k, -k).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the symmetry of scattering data under a nonlocal reduction, first write the reduction on the potential Q(x). Then apply the transformation to the Jost solutions using the appropriate Pauli matrix. The resulting relation on the scattering matrix is almost always of the form S(-k) = C S^T(k) C-1 with a constant matrix C. Memorise the three common C matrices: P for reverse‑space NLS, σ₂ for reverse‑space mKdV, and the 3 × 3 permutation matrix for nonlocal Boussinesq. This pattern recognition saves time and avoids algebraic errors.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Sine‑Gordon Equation

The nonlocal sine‑Gordon equation, uxt = sin u with the reverse‑space‑time reduction u(x, t) = − u(−x, −t), is integrable via a 2 × 2 AKNS‑type Lax pair depending on a spectral parameter λ. The spatial part is Ψx = U Ψ, U = ( i λ /2 ,  ux/4 ;  ux/4 , −i λ /2 ). The nonlocal reduction imposes U(x, t; λ) = − σ₃ U(−x, −t; −λ) σ₃, which yields the symmetry S(−λ) = σ₃ Sᵀ(λ) σ₃ for the scattering matrix. Consequently the reflection coefficient obeys r(−λ) = − r*(λ) and the discrete eigenvalues form quartets (λ, −λ, λ*, −λ*). The inverse problem is a matrix Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real axis with jump matrix built from r(λ) and the exponential factor ei(λx+λ⁻¹t)/2. One‑soliton solutions correspond to a single quartet on the imaginary axis and produce a kink‑antikink pair symmetric about the origin at t = 0. When the quartet contains a complex conjugate pair off the imaginary axis, the solution is a breather that oscillates in time while remaining spatially symmetric. Multi‑soliton solutions are generated by the dressing method with a rational dressing factor that respects the quartet structure. The long‑time asymptotics for decaying initial data are analysed by the Deift–Zhou method; the g‑function is chosen odd in λ to preserve the symmetric jump contour. A notable difference from the local sine‑Gordon equation is that the nonlocal reduction forbids isolated simple poles on the imaginary axis, so pure kink solutions without an accompanying antikink do not exist. This restriction also modifies the phase shift formulas for soliton‑soliton and soliton‑breather interactions.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Davey–Stewartson System

The Davey–Stewartson (DS) system describes the evolution of a complex envelope q(x, y, t) coupled to a real mean field φ(x, y, t). The nonlocal DS‑II equation with reverse‑space reduction q(x, y) = q(−x, −y) and φ(x, y) = φ(−x, −y) admits a Lax pair consisting of a non‑stationary Schrödinger operator L = ∂y − ∂x² − q and a time evolution operator. The direct scattering problem for L at fixed y yields Jost solutions ψ₊, ψ₋ that are scalar functions of x and a spectral parameter k. The nonlocal symmetry forces the scattering data to satisfy R(−k, −y) = R*(k, y) for the reflection coefficient and the discrete eigenvalues to appear in quartets (k, −k, k*, −k*). The inverse problem is a scalar Riemann–Hilbert problem on the real k‑axis with a jump matrix that depends on y and respects the quartet symmetry. One‑soliton solutions are line solitons of the form q(x, y, t) = 2 η sech[η(x + p y − (4 η² + p²) t) + δ] ei(p x − p² y + (η² − p²) t) where the parameters satisfy the nonlocal symmetry, forcing the soliton centre to pass through the origin at t = 0. Multi‑soliton solutions describe elastic interactions of line solitons with modified phase shifts that depend on the transverse wave numbers. The DS system also admits lump solutions rationally localized in both x and y; in the nonlocal case the lump must be symmetric under (x, y) → (−x, −y). The Cauchy problem for decaying initial data is globally well‑posed, and the long‑time asymptotics show soliton resolution into a finite number of line solitons plus a decaying dispersive tail, proved by adapting the Deift–Zhou steepest‑descent method to the symmetric jump contour.

The Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Camassa–Holm Equation

The Camassa–Holm (CH) equation, ut − uxxt + 3 u ux = 2 ux uxx + u uxxx, models shallow water waves and possesses peaked solitons (peakons). Its nonlocal version with reverse‑space reduction u(x) = u(−x) is integrable via a 2 × 2 Lax pair of the form Ψxx = (¼ + λ m) Ψ, where m = u − uxx. The spectral problem is a weighted string equation; the nonlocal symmetry imposes m(x) = m(−x) and forces the scattering data for the associated Sturm–Liouville operator to satisfy R(−λ) = R*(λ) and the discrete eigenvalues to appear in symmetric pairs (± λₙ). The inverse problem is formulated as a Riemann–Hilbert problem on the positive real λ‑axis with a jump matrix built from the reflection coefficient and the exponential e−2√λ x. One‑peakon solutions correspond to a single pair of simple poles and yield the explicit peakon u(x, t) = c e−|x−ct| centred at the origin at t = 0. Multi‑peakon solutions are obtained by solving a finite‑dimensional dynamical system for the peakon positions and amplitudes; the nonlocal reduction forces the peakon configuration to be symmetric, so peakons appear in pairs of equal amplitude moving in opposite directions. The CH equation also admits cuspon and solitary wave solutions; in the nonlocal case these must satisfy the same spatial symmetry. Long‑time asymptotics for decaying initial data are analysed by the method of nonlinear steepest descent for the associated Riemann–Hilbert problem, leading to soliton resolution into a finite number of peakons and a decaying radiation tail. An important technical point is that the spectral problem is not self‑adjoint for the nonlocal CH equation, so the standard spectral theory must be supplemented with a biorthogonal basis to construct the inverse transform.

  • Confusing the symmetry of the scattering matrix for reverse‑space nonlocality (pairs) with that for reverse‑space‑time nonlocality (quartets) leads to an incorrect Riemann–Hilbert formulation.
  • Assuming the nonlocal NLS with non‑zero background has the same one‑soliton formula as the local NLS without imposing the odd g‑function condition.
  • Neglecting the quartet structure of discrete eigenvalues in the nonlocal mKdV and nonlocal sine‑Gordon equations, which is essential for constructing real‑valued soliton and breather solutions.
  • Treating the nonlocal Davey–Stewartson system as a scalar Riemann–Hilbert problem with a jump matrix that ignores the y‑dependence of the reflection coefficient.
  • Overlooking that the nonlocal Camassa–Holm spectral problem requires a biorthogonal basis because the operator is not self‑adjoint; using the standard orthogonal expansion gives wrong norming constants.
  • Forgetting that the nonlocal sine‑Gordon equation admits breather solutions only when the discrete eigenvalues form complex conjugate pairs within the quartet; pure imaginary quartets yield only kink‑antikink pairs.
  • Misapplying the Deift–Zhou steepest descent method to nonlocal equations without modifying the g‑function to be odd (or even) so that the jump contour respects the imposed symmetry.
